    Chapter 1

    Chapter Notes

    Hi everyoneeee!

    Back at it again, huh? I just can't get enough of these two, and I'll keep making it YOUR problem!
    Fun fact: the premise of this fic is heavily inspired by the fact that I have an upstairs neighbor that i really fucking hate because she literally keeps me up almost every night and not because she likes having fun!!!

    Also, Carol is 40 in this and Zosia is 28. Ok that's it, enjoy!!!

    ps. I'm awful at tagging, so they will probably be updated as we go!

    Twenty-four minutes. Almost twenty-five. That’s how long Carol lasts before she crawls out of bed and storms off to the storage room to grab a broom and bang it up against the ceiling. Obviously, the thumping sounds from upstairs are too loud for it to make a difference, and she gives up after her fifth useless try. 

    Oh, how she misses those nights when she took silence and sleep for granted. It’s been two weeks since they have stopped, and the repercussions are starting to show in the sluggish way she moves about in the morning. In the headaches that remind her of the worst kind of hangover. And in the irritation she feels at the slightest inconvenience, or even at her lack of inspiration when she sits in front of a blank document on her computer.

    Well, maybe that last part isn’t entirely someone else’s fault. But still, she should do something about the fucking noise.

    She slips into a robe and huffs, feeling the aggravation surge up to the tips of her fingers. Now, if she were a fully-fledged asshole, like some of her students seem to believe, she’d call the fucking cops and show them right. But she isn’t. In fact, maybe less than ten years ago Carol would’ve been the upstairs neighbor throwing a rager in the middle of the night. But in fucking Albuquerque, New Mexico? Hell no. She’d be somewhere cooler, like Berlin or Barcelona. Kids these days have no idea.

    Carol walks past the mirror on the way to the door, and as she’s about to pull it open, she scurries back to have a look at the state she’s in. Fucking ridiculous. She’s the epitome of the pain-in-the-ass neighbor with five cats and a house that smells like soup, even though she has only one – a fat ugly thing that’s like a hundred years old – and her house doesn’t smell like soup because she can’t cook for shit.

    She tries to make herself look more presentable. Doesn’t understand why, considering that she’s about to get cursed by a bunch of drunk people who are definitely not going to care about her appearance. But still, as she smacks her slippers against each cold concrete step, she thinks that maybe she can endure another sleepless night. That maybe there’s no need for this.

    Just as she’s about to turn the corner and ring the infamous buzzer, the door is thrown open by a tall blonde woman who seems to have popped straight out of a fashion magazine from the early 2000s. Low waist jeans, flimsy top with thin straps, glitter on her chest and gloss so shiny it looks like an entire disco ball.

    She doesn’t acknowledge Carol, merely a fly on the wall for her, and instead turns back to another woman in the doorway. Carol can only see dark hair from where she’s standing, but doesn’t miss a single frame of her grabbing the blonde by the nape of her neck and placing an open-mouthed kiss on her lips. If the music wasn’t so loud, she’d probably hear just how sloppy they’re being, although she doesn’t really have an interest in prolonging this weird impromptu voyeurism session.

    As she settles on turning back and going downstairs, the brunette – still lip-locked to the model – slightly pushes her to the side and against the doorframe. Then, her eyes find Carol’s while her hands slide up the model’s chest, slipping under her top.

    Carol, who in response gasps like someone has just punched her in the ribcage, feels her cheeks burn in embarrassment and stands there, bug-eyed and mouth agape, with absolutely no reason to keep staring. Except that woman smirks into the kiss, and it’s not aimed for the one she’s making out with.

    That look hits a weird spot in Carol’s lower body, and that’s when she glances away and realizes how strange it all was. She doesn’t hear the two women say goodbye, nor is she aware of the model walking past her, squeezing through the space between her and the wall.

    It’s only after a moment that she notices the deafening beat is now more muffled. The woman leans against the closed door and looks at her with something in her eyes that Carol doesn’t know where to place on a scale from curiosity to contempt.

    “Can I help you?” she asks, bringing a cigarette to her mouth and lighting it with a match that ends up in the empty umbrella holder beside the door. Odd to the point of being incredibly annoying. Why not use a lighter like any other fucking normal person?

    “Uhm,” Carol clears her throat and wonders when exactly she’s decided to become so lame. “You can’t smoke in here.”

    That’s all she can say. The woman takes a long drag and strides closer, hips swaying like she’s trying to get something out of it. When she stops in front of Carol, she studies her for a moment before blowing out a cloud of smoke. Carol coughs as it hits her, and that earns her a chuckle that’s neither amused nor amicable. Jury’s verdict says the scales are tipped entirely towards contempt.

    “Oh,” she whispers, close enough that Carol can smell the alcohol in her sultry slur. “Who are you, the smoke police?” She lifts her wrists up and pouts. “Want to arrest me, officer?”

    Carol doesn’t move a muscle, but her heart does pick up a stupid pace at the suggestion.

    “The fuck, no,” she says, snapping out of it and waving her hand to disperse the smoke.

    It’s not that it bothers her. She does smoke from time to time. But this entire situation is idiotic, and she probably should’ve looked up noise-cancelling headphones online instead of trying to reason with her new neighbor who seems to have the utmost disregard for basic societal rules and common sense.

    “You’re going to set off the alarm.”

    “It doesn’t work,” she replies with a shrug. The smoke reaches the ceiling and infiltrates the air vents. Nothing happens. Not even after three minutes, during which Carol sort of hoped she’d be right. It would’ve been a nuisance, having to vacate the building and wait for the all clear from the fire department, but at least she would’ve had that tiny satisfaction. “Told ya. Take it up to the landlord.”

    “Whatever.”

    The door opens again, spilling loud music out in the hallway where Carol really doesn’t want to be anymore. Two women stumble out, giggling and barely managing to stay upright, leaning on each other. They wave goodbye to their host, even though it looks more like they’re both trying to eye-fuck her on the spot.

    What the fuck is this woman’s deal, Carol wonders.

    She doesn’t miss the wink she throws at the taller one as she turns for one last look, and then it’s back to the two of them, smoke hanging heavy in the air. All of a sudden, Carol feels incredibly aware of her state. Messy hair, tired slouch, ugly robe and even uglier slippers. And the other takes it in too, carefully, slowly dragging her narrowed eyes all over Carol’s figure. That makes her clutch the lapels of her robe tighter, trying to hide behind the soft fabric.

    “Well, then? Do you need something?”

    Carol takes a deep breath. For the first time since moving in this awfully sleazy apartment complex, she’s about to be the wet blanket everybody hates – or maybe appreciates for the intervention, she hopes.

    “Yeah,” she mutters and points to the door. “Could you turn it down? I – I’m trying to get some sleep.”

    The other seems to ponder her request for a second before shrugging. “It’s not that loud.”

    “What – Not that loud?” Carol huffs in disbelief. “Not that loud! I’d check my hearing, if I were you.”

    “You seem closer to that point in life than I am.”

    Carol frowns. This woman is unbelievably, unnecessarily and uncomfortably rude, but that seems to be just a hobby for her.

    “Wow, okay. Fine.” She throws her arms in the air. There’s no point in playing such a losing game. “Be a fucking asshole. See where that gets you.”

    She doesn’t wait for another snarky retort, hastily turning around to return to her apartment. Once she’s back downstairs, she slams the door and leans against it, feeling like a kid running home from school after an entire day of getting bullied. Definitely not an unfamiliar sensation, for Carol, but she certainly had put a distance between her and those days.

    Her chest tightens – a mix of anger and frustration – when she hears the thumping bass, almost louder now. Like she’s doing it on purpose. She breathes in deep and closes her eyes, trying to find a place in her mind that might evoke calm, peaceful memories. Maybe a nice beach. The full natural sound of waves breaking gently on the shore.

    Something upstairs falls to the floor with a loud high-pitched crash.

    “Oh my fucking god.”

     

    Carol spends the night reading noise-cancelling headphones reviews and falls asleep with her laptop on her leg. When the alarm abruptly ends the measly two hours of sleep she managed to get, it’s with a jarring sound that pierces straight through her brain. Her laptop falls on the floor with a thud, and she can already feel the headache settling behind her eyelids.

    Getting out of bed and ready for work is a different ball game, and she does it reluctantly, groaning with each step she takes to the bathroom, then to the kitchen.

    While she waits for her moka to heat up, she scrolls on her phone to read the morning news. The usual stuff, nothing encouraging. She does her daily Wordle, and when her espresso is ready she gulps it down like a shot, without even letting it cool a little. Then, she leaves the dirty cup on the counter and grabs her blazer before heading off to work.

    When she opens the door, she almost steps on something placed on her mat. Thankfully, she doesn’t, because getting her expensive leather loafers dirty would’ve been the last straw to a shitty day that’s barely even started.

    She crouches down and eyes the item suspiciously. A tall cup from a local coffee chain. There’s a note under it, but it’s a bit damp and floppy. She takes it anyway and, before reading, takes the lid off the cup and smells its contents. Seems to be a regular black coffee.

    Sorry about the noise yesterday. It will probably happen again  –  Zosia

    Now, Carol likes to think of herself as a reasonable, level-headed person. But a peace offering isn’t really a peace offering if the person making it is this smug, inconsiderate, unrepentant and trying to be a smart-ass about it. At this point it wouldn’t surprise her to find the coffee laced with MiraLAX, which is precisely why not only does she refuse to drink it, but she storms upstairs with a specific purpose. Once she’s standing outside Zosia’s apartment door, she empties the cup of discord into the umbrella holder and tosses the container in there too, for good measure.

    “Asshole,” she mutters as she heads back downstairs.

     

    *

     

    After she’s done with classes for the day, Carol spends a couple more hours in her office receiving all sorts of students: the quiet freshmen who don’t really speak in class, the diligent ones who are looking for thesis advice, the careless types who beg for deadline extensions and come up with the most absurd excuses to get them. Today she’s too tired to hear their reasons and just agrees to give them a couple more days, and they leave thanking her profusely – even though Carol knows they think they’ve played her.

    Once she’s finally alone, she shuts her laptop and leans back in her chair with a loud sigh of relief. She takes off her reading glasses, folds them and sets them on the desk, then she closes her eyes.

    Just for a second.

    The office is pleasantly silent, no chatter outside. Everyone else has long since left, but Carol remains in her comfortable swivel chair, giving it gentle half-spins to the cadence of a quiet lullaby. Her shoulders loosen.

    Maybe a couple minutes.

    When she opens her eyes, the office is dimmer and the peaceful silence from before now feels unsettling and gloomy. She stares at the ceiling, disoriented, and then glances at the clock on the wall.

    Three fucking hours.

    She slept for three hours, although her body doesn’t really count it as rest, because her neck is incredibly stiff and her mouth feels drier than the Sahara desert. But still, this is probably the best sleep she’ll get for the entire week.

    Ever since Zosia moved in the complex, her nights have been nightmarish to say the least. She has the vampiric habit of being incredibly quiet in the mornings and afternoons, but as soon as dusk settles it’s hell unleashed. Furniture scraping against the floor, loud music – sometimes a guitar, sometimes rock records, and then suddenly it’s techno beats and acid grooves blasted all night long with a bunch of people and their shuffling steps and brassy conversations.

    And then, there are some other kinds of nights. The ones where Zosia has someone over and apparently fucks them literally through the floor, because Carol can hear everything – from the way they moan her name to the sounds she makes when she’s close too.

    It had been easy enough to just tune that out for a couple of days. But now she knows what Zosia looks like, and the worst part of it all is that she’s fucking hot. And she really doesn’t want to listen to her hot neighbor fuck women and then hear them leave in the middle of the night, giggling while she walks them downstairs afterwards.

    She has a few options, of course, because the truth is that she doesn’t really need to be living in this apartment. She owns a house that’s been locked and abandoned for almost two years, and if she weren’t such a coward she could still be living there – but it’s not that easy.

    That house is a reminder of something gone forever. It holds a decade of memories, both sweet and painful, that dwell inside every object; in the indentation on the left side of the couch, in the board games buried under signed first editions of her books, and in the pictures framed on the walls. That house is Helen, and she can’t deal with the fact that Helen isn’t here anymore, and hasn’t been for quite some time.

    Which is why – some would say it’s absurd, crazy, wasteful – she’s chosen to rent this lousy apartment instead. Because it’s still better than packing her dead wife’s belongings and having to deal with her empty wardrobe and cold pillow.

    Maybe one day she’ll manage to go back to her house, but not today.

Taking naps that aren’t as satisfying as a full night’s rest always backfires for her, making her feel even more tired than before. But still, between cursing one annoying and slightly misogynistic radio host and some drivers who apparently do not know the definition of a safe distance, she manages to stay awake and get to the apartment safely.

    When she pulls into the reserved parking area, there’s a motorbike in her spot, and she doesn’t even have to guess who it belongs to.

    “You little fucking –”

    She doesn’t have time to finish her thought, thankfully, because it probably would’ve led to her driving straight into that bike to knock it down. And also because Zosia skips out of the building in a leather jacket, dark-washed ripped jeans and combat boots, and walks straight towards Carol’s car, helmet under her arm and a smug smile on her stupid face. Insult to injury, she looks fucking magnificent, as if she doesn’t usually sleep three hours per night.

    Once she’s standing there, Carol eyes her – scowls, really – and doesn’t move a muscle. With a huffed laugh, Zosia taps two fingers on her window. A silent request that Carol really doesn’t want to grant. Yet she rolls it down and raises an eyebrow.

    “What’s up?” She pops the p, hoping to sound casual, but her eyes stay narrow and her lips tight. She doesn’t like being at the mercy of that piercing gaze, and she nervously taps her fingers on the wheel to ground herself and focus on something else.

    “Did I mess up your order?” Zosia speaks as if she has no doubt it’ll make Carol fold. It doesn’t, obviously, but she misses the hint. “I could’ve sworn you were a black coffee kind of girl.”

    Carol shakes her head. “No,” she says curtly, with no further explanation.

    “Then why didn’t you drink it?” Zosia places her helmet on the roof of the car, then leans on it with her forearms. Her face is definitely too close for Carol’s liking.

    “Didn’t ask for it.”

    “You could’ve just thrown it away.”

    “I did,” Carol replies, flat and impatient, before pointing to Zosia’s motorbike. “That’s my spot.”

    Zosia looks back, pursed lips and furrowed brow, then says, “I didn’t see your name on it.”

    “Yeah, well, you don’t even know my name.”

    “Maybe we should fix that, don’t you think?”

Like she’s playing a game, one that has been played countless times before and that she’s grown bored with, and one Carol certainly doesn’t want to entertain. So, she lets Zosia’s question hang and rolls up the window, tapping her index finger against her ear.

    “Sorry, I can’t hear you,” she says as the glass finally puts her at a distance. Zosia pushes herself off the car, slow and unhurried, and reaches for her helmet. Then, she glances one last time toward Carol and rolls her eyes, although now she definitely seems amused. Or maybe she’s just making fun of her, plotting ways to disrupt her days even more.

Which means that she’ll finally get a fucking chance at sleeping without having to bury her head under three pillows.

She practically flies upstairs, once she’s parked her car in her spot – which, by the way, doesn’t need to have her name on it, because everybody knows it’s hers – and runs herself a nice hot bath. She lights up a few candles, pours a full glass of red wine and sips at it while listening to a ten-hour compilation of relaxing music and rain sounds. When the bathtub is almost overflowing with bubbles, she refills her glass and slips in the steaming water. It sloshes around just like the wine in her glass, threatens to spill and then settles in a comfortable stillness.

    The warmth all around her seems to work wonders on the tightness in her spine and neck, and she massages the taut muscle until she feels the knots there loosen. A sigh of relief escapes her lips.

Her eyelids feel droopy, and before she risks falling asleep with her head half submerged, she removes the stopper and lets the tub drain, rinses off with the hand-shower and gets out. Although she probably should’ve been more sensible and avoid drinking this much in the middle of the week, sometimes she just can’t be bothered to care. So, she grabs the leftover wine and downs it straight from the bottle.

It wasn’t that big of a deal when Helen was there and kept an eye out for her, telling her enough, baby when she  started to stumble and sound incoherent. Not a tough feat, since Zosia seems completely absorbed in the conversation she’s having on the phone.

    She stops, listening to what whoever’s on the line has to say.

Honestly, not even being drunk is a good enough reason to explain why she’s standing there like she needs to know the rest of it. None of this concerns her – Zosia’s life, loud and irritating, and her refusal to go somewhere because of someone shouldn’t really register as anything of relevance.

The movement makes the dim light catch on the sharp line of her jaw, on the thin silver ring hooked through her ear. Carol realizes she’s staring, again, and doesn’t pay enough attention to the way she drops the bag in the metallic trashcan. It resonates with a loud thud, and that’s enough to ruin her plans of lurking in the shadows.

    “Hey,” she says with a smirk. It doesn’t feel light-hearted and cheeky like the others, but it does have the distinct effect on Carol of making her stomach drop a little. “Were you planning some sort of revenge on me, neighbor?”

    “Oh, nice,” Zosia replies, eyes still glued to Carol’s. She doesn’t let go of her gaze even as she fumbles with something in the pocket of her jacket. “You know, I have something that goes perfectly with that.” She takes out a rectangular metal tin, which she pops open to reveal a few pre-rolled joints. She slips one between her lips, then lights it with one of her stupid matches, and Carol is enraptured by those long fingers deftly moving like they’ve done this a million times. Somehow, she ends up focusing on her short nails, and she swallows so audibly she’s sure Zosia’s noticed.

She remembers one of the last times she smoked weed, on a fucking camping trip with Helen for her thirtieth birthday. She’d wanted them to do something unusual, and Carol hadn’t had the heart to admit that she’d hate every second of it, so she went anyway only to end up high as a kite in a massive tent, crying because she was convinced they were about to be mauled by bears.

    Somehow, that memory still isn’t enough of a deterrent, and she has to forcefully shake off the temptation and stop herself from reaching for the joint.

    “I don’t think that’s a good idea,” she replies instead as she moves her hand back to her side.

    Zosia shrugs and doesn’t say anything. Now, the air hangs heavy between them, thick with the smell of weed, and a chill gust of wind makes Carol shiver.

    “Okay, then. I’ll, uhm, leave you to it,” she blurts out, awkwardly pointing over her shoulder. But before she leaves, Carol asks one last stupid, lame question. “Oh, wait. Are you by any chance planning to have a party tonight? Or maybe rearrange your living room?”

    Zosia chuckles and makes a scene of thinking about it. “No, I don’t think so,” she says, tapping a finger against  her chin. Then, a lazy and infuriating grin plays on her lips. “But I was thinking I should find a new spot for the couch. Want to come up and help me figure it out? Test out a few angles?”

    Carol chokes on her spit and coughs to make it sound casual. “What? No. Just, whatever you do, keep it down, please?”

    “Yeah, yeah, got it.”

    “Oh. That was easy.”

    “Not in the mood to argue tonight,” Zosia says, blowing out more thick smoke, and turning to the side to look at Carol with a quirked eyebrow. “Disappointed? Were you expecting a bit of a fight?”

    “I – uhm.” Carol shakes her head. “Sort of.”

    “Next time, maybe.”

There have been looks – subtle, but also not really – that she’s still feeling on her skin right now. And it’s the second time that she’s had Zosia study her as if she was just waiting for the right moment to bite, have a taste, and then move on to the next prey.

    She keeps thinking about those dark eyes – the way they moved up her body, paused like they were taking notes, and finally settled on her face. And it was such a magnetic energy that she can’t shake it off long after she’s locked herself inside her apartment.

    There’s someone else in her apartment. Carol knows it by listening to the steps that follow each other and keep a distinct cadence – rubber soles and clacking heels. Turns out that, after two weeks of hearing someone walk over your head, you learn to discern their patterns. So, where Zosia’s steps are light, sometimes slightly dragged, this new unknown pair is slow and uncertain, but heavier in the way it lands against the floor.

    She hears muffled voices – a conversation, first, of which she can’t make out words, but then the inflection becomes unmistakable. And among the banging sounds against the wall and the high-pitched moans, she hears a woman cry Zosia’s name so loud it sounds like they’re practically fucking in Carol’s bed.

    *

    “So what should I do?”

    Manousos tosses the rag on the counter and scratches the nape of his neck. “I can’t tell you what to do, man,” he says, and then he sighs. Carol feels like he’s past the point of regretting coming over. “Just – if you care about her, in any capacity, don’t let her go. Hold on to the good things.”

     

    *

     

    After smoking two cigarettes in a row, Zosia walks back into the kitchen reeking of burnt tobacco and bad decisions. The yellowed tip of her index finger is disgusting, but then again, chain-smoking isn’t the stupidest thing she’s ever done to take her mind off the bleak reality of things.

    Carol and Manousos are putting plates and glasses away, and Zosia wonders whether they’ve talked it out and they’re simply collaborating peacefully or they’re giving each other the silent treatment out of spite. Either way, she thinks back to her brief exchange with him.

    She should really tell Carol that she’s leaving.

    “Hey,” she says, popping in between the two of them. “Can I help?”

    “It’s fine,” Carol briefly looks back at her. “We got this.”

A painting with a very straightforward title, something Seurat-esque like Two women on a couch and a cat, and everything they’re hiding for the observer to unravel.

    “Oh, so you did hear it.”

    “I did,” Zosia admits cheekily. Her heart starts beating out of her chest, because this is familiar territory, this is somewhere she knows how to safely exist in. “I just chose to ignore it.”

    “Why would you even do that?”

    Right, reasons. Carol always wants to know the reason why anyone does anything. Sometimes, Zosia would just love to yell in her face, to tell her to stop being so focused on why things happen and get her brains working toward figuring out who is making the effort to keep them running. Besides, it’s not like she has a good explanation to give her. It’s just that Carol is fascinating to her, always has been, even when Zosia spotted her by the trashcans and she didn’t notice, or when she left for work forgetting something and walking right back in with an annoyed frown on her face, muttering unkind words to herself.

    Thank fuck for that, Zosia thinks, but then she also starts dreading the moment he’ll leave them alone. Like it’s a fucking swim in the open sea with sharks going in circles around her.

     

    *

     

    When Manousos leaves, they remain on the couch, plunged into renewed silence, like they don't know where to pick up from. Should they just keep turning pages until Zosia is too bored to pay attention? Should they talk about fucking in the backyard and then not being able to look into each other’s eyes?     But, at first, she doesn’t say anything, and predictably Zosia gets distracted by the various states of intoxication Carol’s been immortalized in: 2018 Carol, drunk on Chardonnay that she spilled on her shirt. 2019 Carol, donning a drinking helmet and cheering on people taking part in a hot dog eating competition. 2020 Carol, socially distanced, wearing a mask and slipping a straw under it.

    “That’s commitment,” Zosia comments, flipping another page only to find it empty.

    “I know, right?” Carol says with a lukewarm smile, then she shakes her head. “Some would call it a problem.”

    Zosia purses her lips and doesn’t say anything.

To just, once again, ignore common sense and give in to the undeniable pull that’s always there, never yielding, never giving her peace. The thing that works between them, something Zosia should’ve probably settled for.

    But fuck it – she doesn’t want to settle for unattached sex, no matter how good it feels to come on Carol’s fingers, to feel her inside of her and to watch her efforts to push her to new heights each time.     They speak and stumble over each other, unsure about whose job it is to fill the silence.

    “You first,” Carol says with a soft, kind gesture of her hand.

    Zosia swallows. It’s hard, like she’s choking down on jagged rocks. Her pulse thunders into her ears, and she doesn’t even hear the words that come out of her mouth, but Carol’s face drops and she knows it was the worst thing she could’ve said to her tonight, or ever.

     

    *

     

    “I’m leaving in three days.”

    Carol blinks. It’s compulsory, like she’s trying to cast away the hurt from her face. Zosia shrinks in her spot on the couch, and they’re not touching anymore.

    I’m leaving in three days.

    It doesn’t sound like a chill I’ll be back next week, come get me at the airport. It sounds like a stupid I’m leaving and I won’t be back.

    And it’s exactly what Carol knew would happen so, once again, circumstances prove her right. She wonders if that warrants a celebratory lap and yelling a petty told you so to Manousos. Fucking hell, he almost got her through with his lovey-dovey bullshit about trusting people and not letting them go.

    “Okay,” Carol says, then she gets up on autopilot and grabs the bottle they’d left on the coffee table. “Do you need me to drive you to the airport or something?”

    “No, I –” Zosia stands too, and now they’re facing off in the middle of the living room where Carol fought countless times with her wife, and still none of them felt as ugly as that one simple statement. Because Helen always chose to stay, except for that one time she had no say in it. “That’s it?”

    “What do you mean that’s it?”

    “You were about to say something.”

    “It doesn’t matter.”

    Carol doesn’t wait for Zosia to reply and leaves the room, walking out into the backyard. She plops down into an old deck chair and finds that she can’t stand to look at the ruined patches of grass, so she averts her gaze. Zosia’s cigarettes are on the wrought iron table to her left. She grabs one and lights it.

    Things will turn out fine, she thinks as she looks up to the starry sky. They always do, eventually.

     

     

     

    Things don’t turn out fine. Zosia tries to talk to her, and Carol dismisses her because she doesn’t want to hear about a sudden change of heart, so she ends up watching her leave the next day. Her assumption is just that she’ll spend the night at Koumba’s place, but she doesn’t ask.

    But then, panic settles in as Zosia’s bike disappears from view and she can’t hear its low rumble anymore, not even the echo.

    Things don’t turn out fine, and Carol wants to punch her own stupid face for doing everything in her power to make sure it wouldn’t happen.

     

    July

     

    Carol has a hard time sleeping in that big, empty house. It’s been weeks and she still tosses and turns and hopes to feel some warmth on the other side of her bed, although the nights have been unbearably hot, and she hates it. Maybe that factors in her insomnia, all the irritation and sweat.

    In the mornings, she runs errands and repopulates that dead place with things she had to leave at her apartment – still inaccessible until further notice – and are probably ruined anyway. A new laptop to work on the draft she was supposed to show Val, a cat tree tower for Nacho, some clothes so that she can feel like a human being and not a slug clad in sweats all day long. She also buys a new vibrator, just in case she gets the urge through the sadness, and DVDs. A shit ton of DVDs.

    A Golden Girls box set, all the Shrek and Saw movies, When Harry met Sally, The Texas Chainsaw Massacre, Notting Hill, Dawn of the Living Dead, The Princess Bride, The NeverEnding Story. The cabinet under her TV becomes a den of unclear taste and indecisiveness, and she ends up watching movies back to back until they all blend together. So, when Sally is boasting her fake orgasm in the diner, she suddenly has flashes of people cutting off their limbs to escape Jigsaw’s traps.

    Carol thinks about it. If she were trapped in one of those sadistic puzzles, she probably wouldn’t fight. She’d watch the minutes tick by on the clock, because she’d rather croak than meticulously carve holes in her body to weigh pounds of her own flesh. It does prove one thing: she’s a coward through and through, even when Manousos comes over for dinner and asks her about things and she avoids talking about how she’s feeling now that she hasn’t seen or heard from Zosia in fourteen days. She doesn’t even try to pretend that she doesn’t care. She just doesn’t acknowledge the subject, as if that’s any different.

    Manousos knows it, and after the first few times, he stops prying and sits with her as they rewatch horror flicks and crack a smile at how ridiculous some of them are. It’s incredibly dramatic, but it feels like Carol is relearning life, or rather resetting it to what she knew before Zosia, which is what she knew after Helen.

    It’s hard as fuck, especially when Zosia tries to call and Carol just flips her phone face down on the couch until it stops. And then she dreams of her – and the shame of waking up wet and masturbating to a projection of her that hasn’t left settles deep in Carol's chest for the rest of the day.

    As days go by, Carol realizes that she’s lost that game of chicken. Thinking about Zosia, dreaming of her, seeing her in corners of her house she’s never set foot in – those are well-deserved consequences, and she keeps eating shit and savoring it every time she decides not to do anything about it.

    She buys mango ice cream and listens to Fleetwood Mac on CD, slamming the old boombox when Go your own way comes on and she thinks of Zosia and hates that, even when presented with the opportunity of doing it, Carol didn’t give her her world. Zosia would’ve definitely taken it from her.

     

     

    The eerie dullness of her lonely summer days comes to a sudden stop when, on a Monday morning, she sits at her desk and stares at her inbox, sorting through spam emails, and finds one at the bottom of the list from the previous week. She doesn’t recognize the sender address and there’s no subject, which normally would be enough for Carol to trash it without even thinking about it. But today, something feels different, and she clicks on the message, hoping that it’s not a scam or a malware or whatever they’re called.

    It’s not a malware. It doesn’t even feel like a scam, unless scammers have somehow planted bugs in her old apartment and know that Zosia was supposed to get her some sort of pass for the show she’s playing in Chicago – in exactly ten days.

    “Got you a guest pass for the show. You can come get it or not, I don’t care, but I’m sure she does.”

    Carol feels her heart drop to her ass. She reads the email once, twice, then clicks away from it for a few seconds before she goes back to squinting at the message. It’s got to be Natalie, there’s no other logical explanation. Zosia wouldn’t send her something like this and pretend to be someone else.

    But then, that begs another question: is Zosia talking about her to her bandmates – notably her ex and the woman she got cheated on with – or did Carol fuck her up so badly that she doesn’t even need to mention her for them to take such an odd initiative? Would she really be happy to see her? And, if she were to take this chance that’s being served to her on a silver platter, would things really work out differently between them?

    She can’t answer that question, and that makes her panic, because it would be pretty fucking stupid to waste an opportunity to make amends when all she’s been doing these days is sitting on her couch and regretting every decision that led her to this self-inflicted loneliness.

    The truth is that she misses Zosia. She’d missed her the first time, and she misses her now that she knows what to call these feelings. Now that they’re eating at her heart and she feels like the idiot that had to break every single bone in her body to understand that each one has a function.

    And obviously the heart isn’t a bone, but she still hopes Zosia’s isn’t damaged beyond repair.

    She slams her laptop shut, throws on a clean t-shirt (one that isn’t stained with wine) and heads out to her car.

     

    -

    “Claro, por favor, pasa, pasa,” he mutters under his breath. “Que total, no estaba haciendo nada importante.”

    “Dude, how about we take it down a notch?” Carol replies, but it sounds more like a ridiculous whine. She reaches for a cookie just to have something to do with her hands. “I’m an idiot, you were right. You don’t need to keep reminding me.”

    “Well, took you long enough.”

    “I know. It’s – uhm,” Carol feels a familiar warmth creep up her neck. If she owes Zosia an apology for not stopping her, then surely Zosia owes her one for not staying. The logic falls apart the second she looks at it too closely. It all leads back to one single cause – infatuation, affection, love, or whatever she’s bold enough to call it on a good day. And it feels like if she keeps bottling things up, she’ll just explode and fucking die.

    So she either dies a miserable, indolent wretch – on her couch with Cheeto fingers and people being slaughtered on her TV – or she decides to stop being a spectator to her own disgrace and swerves in the right direction, for once in her life.

    That direction might be Zosia. Or it might not. But if there’s a tiny chance that something good comes out of it, maybe she shouldn’t question it as much as she’s been doing.

    She clears her throat before answering Manousos’ question and hopes for the best – which, in this case, would be saying what she needs to say without her voice cracking. “Do you remember when Helen died –” First crack. She keeps going, nonetheless. “And you came over and made sure I ate and took care of myself and basically kept functioning as a human being? And you kept doing it even when I snapped at you and yelled and acted fucking crazy.”

    Manousos nods, lips sealed into a thin line and a focused expression on his face.

    “I never thanked you for that.” Second crack. “I think you’re one of the reasons I’m still here today.”

    “What – Carol. You don’t have to thank me.”

    “No, I do. Just take it and don’t make it weird,” Carol lifts a finger to shut him up. “Anyway. Lately, I feel like we’ve been replaying those days front to back, minus the death. Well, symbolically you could say that I still lost something. Not that it’s the same thing, but Zosia leaving after dropping a fucking bomb on me made me angrier than it should’ve, because I knew what was happening all along, and fuck, I hoped for it. I hoped she’d love me. And look where that got me.”

    “You can’t water a flower and be surprised when it blooms.”

    “Wow. That’s some fucking fortune cookie bullshit right there, man.”

    “Premium Oviedo wisdom. Go on before I kick you out.”

    “I should’ve handled it better. When she said she was falling for me, I knew I could’ve said it back. And right there I didn’t know what got into me. Maybe I was pissed that she didn’t wait a little longer. Two more months and I could’ve been the first to say it, and that would’ve been on my own terms.”

    “That’s a little selfish, though.”

    “I know it is. I have a lot to make up for. So, an apology. It’s a good start, right?”

    “If you don’t half-ass it, sure,” Manousos nods and removes his apron, folds it neatly and places it on the counter. “Now, if you must know, I’m about to have someone over –”

    “Dude, what?”

    “It’s just a friend.”

    “You don’t have friends.”

    “No, you don’t. So, you go get your girl, dumbass. Write down your big speech, channel your inner Raban or whatever. I’m sure she’ll give you another chance.”

    “Does that mean you’re cat-sitting Nacho until I’m back? Oh, and can I get some cookies before I go?”

     

    -

     

    Five days later, Carol is pacing in her studio chewing off the cap of her whiteboard marker, occasionally glancing at the bullet point list of things she has to make up for.

    Point number one, shutting Zosia out. Point number two, lying by omission about her own feelings. Point number three, being a short-tempered, indecisive asshole. It still feels like she’s missing so much.

    From time to time, her phone buzzes and she ignores it – there’s only two people who might want to talk to her. One and a half, if she considers the possibility that part of Zosia hates her and is calling and/or texting just as a reminder of what she’s missing out on while she’s touring, playing her music and having the time of her life. So, she focuses on the task at hand: making sure that she’s got way more reasons for Zosia to forgive her than she does for hating her.

    It’s not that easy, because Carol is nothing if not extremely cruel to herself, but she still tries her best.

     

    August

     

    Val is waiting for her at the literary café where they’ve been meeting for the past ten years – a cozy, unassuming spot that survived the gradual depressing extinction of independent bookstores, and that also happens to be one of the obligatory stops for Carol when she’s on a book tour. It’s got a capacity of barely twenty people at once, and it always gets weirdly intimate when readers start asking questions, but it feels real, palpable. Humble, even. The opposite of flying too close to the sun.

    “Carol!” Val waves from a table in the corner, calling her over with more enthusiasm than Carol thinks she deserves. She even stands, hands flying up to Carol’s shoulders to pull her into a hug, then plants two kisses on her cheeks and finally sits, flashing a bright smile. “You made it!”

    Carol really can’t believe anyone would be that happy to see her.

    “Yeah, sorry I’m late,” she replies, a bit out of breath from the warm welcome and also because she keeps insisting on wearing a tie for these occasions – a mix of superstition, tradition and stupidity – no matter how hot it is outside. “Traffic was awful.”

    Val shrugs. “It’s fine,” she says, and when a waitress comes over to their table to take their order, she replies with a kind the usual, before turning her attention back to Carol with a short laugh. “At least you didn’t ditch me again.”

    “I knew you’d hold it against me,” Carol says, hand on her chest in feigned indignation. “You know I almost died in a fire that day, right?”

    “No, you didn’t.”

    “Yeah, no, I didn’t.” Carol laughs, and then she takes out of her bag a printed copy of the first chapters of her new Wycaro book and slides it across the table. “I survived just so I could hand over my precious craft to you.”

    “Oh, great,” Val says, flipping through some pages. “So, should I smack you now or after I’ve read it?”

    “What?”

    “You know I hate it when you talk shit about your work.”

    “I didn’t say any –”

    “Didn’t need to,” Val clicks her tongue. She remains silent for a few minutes, her eyes skimming over the first page of the draft. “You’re lucky I’ve always liked you, or with this attitude you’d still be looking for a publisher for Winds.”

    “Well, it’s all thanks to Helen,” Carol says, her name slipping past her lips without warning. Val seems surprised by it. “You know she made me more likeable.”

    “Come on, you’re not that bad.”

    “Debatable.”

    Val shrugs and dismisses her with a wave of her hand, still smiling through it all. When the waitress brings their coffees over, she moves the folder to the side and grabs her cup, gently blowing on it. “So,” she says, before taking a sip. “How have you been?”

    “Oh. You know.”

    Val gives her a look. “No, I don’t. That’s why I asked.”

    “I’ve been alright.”

    “Do better,” Val huffs, then picks the manuscript back up. She reads a few more pages while sipping her coffee, and Carol assumes this is her way of giving her space and time to come up with a better answer, but then she watches as she lifts her eyebrows and her eyes almost pop out of their sockets, and she gets a little self-conscious about it all. “Woah. Carol.”

    “Uhm. What.”

    “This – this is good. Like, really good. I mean, it’s unexpected, but it still feels so natural,” Val goes off, an excited smile breaking out on her lips. “Raban as a woman? It’s – shit, it’s genius.”

    Carol blinks. “Are you fucking with me?”

    “Why would I? This is brilliant.”

    “What if my readers hate it and it flops?”

    “Carol,” Val exhales, like she’s a mother about to give a pep talk to her son before his basketball game. “You wrote this for a reason. If your regular readers don’t get it, then you’ll find another audience that will value that reason and be grateful for it. Besides, you can afford to take some risks.”

    Carol doesn’t want to tell Val that the reason has a name, and beautiful brown eyes, and the kindest, most annoying smile she’s ever seen on a person. She doesn’t want to tell her that the reason is a woman she has feelings for, someone who, just like Helen, albeit unknowingly, pushed her toward a change that she felt too weak to face on her own.

    And Carol certainly doesn’t want to tell her that she’ll have to cut their meeting short because she has to run to the airport and catch a fucking plane to get to her in hopes that she’ll accept an apology she still isn’t sure how to word.

    “Earth to Carol,” Val calls, leaning forward and waving her hand in Carol’s face. “You still with me?”

    “Huh. Yeah.”

    “Are you actually worried about sales? Because if you are you know the marketing –”

    Carol shakes her head before Val can finish her sentence. “No. I guess that was just an excuse. I’ve been making a shit ton of them lately.”

    “Okay, so we’re not talking about Wycaro anymore.”

    So, again, Carol doesn't want to tell Val about her reason. But she does it anyway, and realizes that nobody had been holding her inside her loneliness, grief, and bitterness more tightly than she had.

     

    -

     

    When she leaves the café, with the promise of catching up soon and introducing Zosia to Val – provided that she doesn’t fuck it up in Chicago – Carol sits in her car and takes off her linen blazer. She’s sweating, she looks like a depressed accountant on the verge of doing something stupid, but, for the first time in a month, she feels like she has everything under control.

    There’s a duffel bag in the back of her car, a little token of hope and optimism. It might seem stupidly pointless, but she’ll take it over dying of anxiety at the thought of Zosia possibly telling her to go fuck herself.

    Helen used to say that she was always better at running from things than running toward them.

    Carol remembers laughing when she said it the first time, like it was a joke Helen had aimed at everyone else in the room except her. Helen hadn’t laughed back, of course, and she’d just looked at her with that calm, tired patience she offered when she wanted Carol to know that she’d still be by her side, that she wouldn’t go anywhere despite it all.

    It worked, eventually. Carol learned to stand still, which was progress, compared to fleeing, but it still wasn’t good enough. She gets it now. Watching things, days, life go by and doing nothing to be a part of it might be even worse, in a way.

    So, she’s finally reacting. Exposing herself to the renewed possibility of being left and hurt, the ontological truth of being alive.

    She turns the key in the ignition and starts the car, taking in a deep breath. Then, before setting off for the airport, she turns the radio on. It’s set on a local station.

    “Hello Albuquerque!” says the radio show host after a dragged jingle. “We’re back at it! I’m your friendly host Max Welton and this is The B-Side Shift, live on The Edge! Boy, we got some real gems for you today!”

    Carol sighs. Max is way too cheerful for the turmoil she’s feeling inside. It’s quite offensive. She pulls out of her parking spot into the busy road – panic settling in as soon as she realizes that she should’ve left sooner.

    “Shit,” she mutters at a red light.

    “First things first. Are you stuck in traffic?”

    Carol frowns. “What the fuck, Max.”

    “Going crazy because you might miss that date, that deadline, that train that might change your life, Sliding Doors style? Don’t worry, we got you. Sometimes you just gotta take things as they come, am I right? So, let’s all take a trip down memory lane. You know, early 2000s energy, instant serotonin. If you’ve ever wanted to feel like you could fix your life in under three minutes, this one’s for you. Extra points if you roll down your window and sing your heart out to this anthem!”

    “No way I’m rolling my windows down, Max. It’s ninety degrees outside.”

    There’s a beat of silence before –

    “Somebody once told me the world is gonna roll me, I ain’t the sharpest tool in the shed…”

    Carol shakes her head, but she still smiles and ends up rolling her window down, just a tiny bit, humming along to the song until Max comes back on and says something stupid about the weather.

     

    -

     

    The airport is as menacing as ever for someone like Carol, who’d rather set foot in a shark tank than on a plane. Duffel bag slung on her shoulder, sleeves rolled up and tie loose around her neck, she’s sweating like she’s being baptized as she wades through the crowd and goes through security checks. She isn’t hiding a bomb under her folded underwear, but somehow the tall guy standing in front of her makes her feel like she does.

    After her bag has been scanned, no explosive in sight, Carol locates her gate with her heart pounding in her chest. If anything, that feels like a fucking ticking bomb, and today of all days she had to forget her beta blockers at home. So, if her heart gives out and she drops dead in the middle of the airport, it’s entirely on her stupidity.

    Before she boards the plane, she gets a text from Manousos. It’s a simple thumbs up and an encouraging “you got this”. She types a quick reply (I feel like I’m going to die but thank you), then switches to airplane mode and slips her phone back into her pocket.

     

    The flight is uneventful, thankfully. She sits in first class, gives in to the full businessman treatment when a beautiful flight attendant approaches her with a smile and a glass of whiskey. She even tops her off when she notices that the glass is empty, but when she comes by a second time, Carol puts her hand over it, returns the smile and shakes her head. She really can’t afford to get to Chicago drunk off her ass.

     

    -

     

    After she lands in Chicago, Carol takes a transfer from the airport and gets to the hotel she booked just in time to check in, leave her bag and then sprint out again to get to Grant Park. She doesn’t care that she looks messed up, sweaty and quite desperate, because she’s already running late, and she needs to get to the festival before she misses that thirty minute window of time allotted to Zosia’s show.

    Traffic, once again, is screwing her plans over. She pulls her phone out while she sits in the back of her Uber, stuck in a jam, and checks the route on her Maps app. Her right leg won’t stop bouncing as she stares at the red line redrawing itself. No one’s moving, people blow their horns and yell at each other, and Carol will be late if she doesn’t do something about it.

    She studies her surroundings.

    “Hey,” she says, tapping her driver’s shoulder. “I’m going to get off here, okay?”

     

    -

     

    Carol doesn’t normally do reckless things, but desperate times call for desperate measures. She weaves through cars, drivers honking at her, and prays that they don’t run her over or, in the likely event that they do, that she gets away with a minor injury.

    But all she can think about is Zosia, and the little blue dot on her phone screen getting closer to her destination. She takes turns, crosses roads, ignores red lights and almost bumps into a woman with her stroller.

    “Sorry!” She yells, looking back but still running, running, running like she’s being chased.

    She mutters words of encouragement mixed with insults aimed at herself as her lungs burn in protest and her legs ache, but then she finally spots festival signs and vendor tents in the distance, and she could almost cry with relief because she's close.

    Fuck, she wishes she had time to stop for a cold beer. Instead, she has to somehow find a way to get in with only a snarky email from one of the performers and no actual wristband on her arm.

    The closer she gets to the entrance, the more obvious it becomes that nobody is getting through without credentials. Wristbands flash everywhere around her – bright colors waved in lanes checked by security.

    “Shit.”

    Carol slows down long enough to catch her breath and approaches a woman in a festival polo standing beside a barricade.

    "Hi," she pants, trying not to sound like she's seconds away from a medical emergency, even though the shirt sticking to her back and her heaving chest say otherwise. "I think I'm supposed to have a guest pass? I don’t – I know it sounds fake, but, uhm, I have – I have an email.”

    The girl looks at her as though it’s not the first time she’s seen something like this. “Artist guest?”

    “Shit. I – uhm. Yeah, I guess. Maybe?”

    Then, it seems like the universe finally takes pity on her, it aligns just perfectly so as the festival worker points her to Guest Services, and she gets to that area, talks to people, says her name fifteen times, shows her ID, the email Natalie sent her, almost passes out because she doesn’t even breathe to tell whoever’s listening that she has to get in – and then a big bald guy with a shiny burn on his head and square sunglasses comes by with a wristband and pats her back.

    “Hey, chill,” he laughs. Carol can’t see it, but she’s sure he’s rolling his eyes as he secures the band on her wrist. “You’re in.”

    Carol can’t even thank him, because she’s so stressed out she’d probably cry if she starts speaking again. Fucking cortisol spikes.

    “By the way, my wife loves your books,” the man says. “Can we take a quick picture?”

     

    -

     

    Carol takes that fucking photo. But then someone else sees the guy taking a picture with her and mistakes her for someone important, so they ask for one as well, and she ends up taking five more. They’ll probably wind up on the Internet, a laughable legacy, but she doesn’t care.

    All she cares about now is getting to Zosia, and she’ll bulldoze through the crowd to make sure she’s there in time. She doesn’t even remember what she wants to say to her – it’s like her body has been reduced to primary motor functions, no speech whatsoever.

    But then she makes it to the BMI Stage and there’s already a large crowd gathered there. Carol didn’t know what she was expecting, but it definitely wasn’t that.

    She’d imagined something smaller. A handful of people, fifty tops, like the venues she played in Albuquerque. The ones in which she demanded Carol’s gaze be on her all the time. A nostalgic thrill runs down her spine at the thought, but then again, even if Carol tried, Zosia would never spot her in a crowd like this.

    She looks around for an opening, doesn’t find it and gives up. She’ll watch her from a distance, maybe try and get to the backstage once the show is done. It would be incredibly selfish too, to pop up in there now that Zosia’s about to go on stage, unsure of what her reaction would be.

    So, Carol stands still. Like the rest of these people, she waits and fans herself, complains about the heat, and catches herself staring toward the wings of the stage until there’s finally some movement. A flash of dark hair and she’s immediately on her toes, heart beating out of her chest, palms sweaty.

    Zosia is fixing her in-ear, nodding as someone talks to her. She turns a few tuning pegs and strums her guitar twice before she finally comes out and skips to the center of the stage. Natalie and Jules follow, and there’s a fourth woman she doesn’t know, but honestly, Carol barely registers their presence.

    Her eyes are on Zosia, glued to her every move. She’s mesmerizing, glowing, and Carol can tell she’s trying not to smile too bright – she has to act cool, after all. Like this is just a day’s work for her. But then she actually plays like this is just another day’s work, and Carol has to remember that she’s been doing this for a while. Still, she feels some sort of pride at the sight.

    From conversations in diners about not feeling the connection to her music, to playing original songs on a fucking festival stage. She gets a little emotional like she has any claim over it.

    After a couple of songs with energetic fuzzy guitars that made everyone jump and clash against each other, Zosia slows down. Natalie and Jules keep playing a mellow beat while she speaks over it, looking at people in the crowd as if she knows them personally.

    “How’s it going, Chicago?” She asks, lips against the microphone. Then, with her usual sultry disposition, she sets into the flirty act. “God, you look so beautiful from up here. Every single one of you.”

    The crowd cheers loudly. Carol nods and smiles, throat too dry to even let out a sound.

    “Speaking of beautiful people,” Zosia continues, winking and pointing to someone to the front, then gently strumming her guitar to accompany her words. “How many of you are in love right now? Show of hands. Come on, don’t be shy.”

    She waits for the crowd to erupt once again, louder than before, as if they’d been waiting for permission to admit such a thing.

    “Or, not even love. Let’s just say you’re into someone. Maybe it’s the person right next to you, maybe not,” she goes on, and the beat picks up to something familiar. Carol is fucking sure she’s hummed this song a bunch of times before, when Zosia was around. “Either way, they feel like home, don’t they?”

    The guy standing next to Carol yells, louder than anyone else, a yeah that makes Zosia chuckle.

    “I like the enthusiasm,” she says, scanning the crowd until her gaze lands on him. But then she shifts it just a tiny bit, and freezes for a second, mouth hanging open against the mic.

    Her eyes lock in with Carol’s.

    For the hundredth time today, Carol feels like she’s barely able to stand on her feet.

    “The world stops when you’re with them,” Zosia continues, a slight shake in her voice as she looks away. “And you’re not sure whether you found them, or they found you. But you’re grateful anyway, because you’ve found your place. So, if you get the feeling, this one's for you.”

    The synth swells up right there, bass and drums follow, and Zosia shifts into singing about being an animal looking for a home, and Carol just can’t look away.

     

    -

     

    After the show, Carol wades through people commenting on the performance, appreciating Zosia’s voice, skills and looks – which pisses her off just a tiny, healthy bit – and people claiming that she’d been looking at them for the entirety of the show.

    Ah, Carol thinks, you wish.

    The closer she gets to the stage, the tighter her stomach feels. Her tongue is like hard sandstone in her mouth, but she pushes through until security stops her and she hastily waves her wristband to get through.

    She doesn’t make it too far up the back, because Natalie is rushing toward her like she wants to beat her ass.

    “You made it,” she says as she stares at her with something akin to disgust. “Don’t tell Zosia I did this.”

    “Well, who else could’ve done it?” Carol asks with a frown. “I don’t –”

    “I don’t know. Be vague. Deflect. I don’t care,” Natalie replies, stopping her with a wave of her hand. “But I don’t want to be responsible if you fuck this up.”

    “Uhm. Okay. Wow. Thanks.”

    “Fuck, you look like you’re straight out of the Great Depression. Some Big Crash shit. You could’ve at least tried.”

    Carol looks down to her clothes. Natalie isn’t wrong, and that’s incredibly aggravating.

    “Right, fuck you too, Natalie,” she snarks, moving past her. Before she rounds the corner to keep looking for Zosia, she turns and mutters a reluctant, “Thank you.”

    Natalie doesn’t say anything.

    The backstage area is narrower than she thought. There’s ATA trunks all over the scuffed black flooring, taped down cables and a low buzz that’s equal parts adrenaline from people and feedback from guitars. Someone briskly walks past her, muttering something into a headset.

    Carol sees Zosia before she notices her. She’s leaning with her back against a stack of cases, guitar still strapped across her, hair damp at the temple. It’s shorter than before, and god, Carol knew it already, but she looks even more beautiful up close. She seems at ease despite the tired sag in her shoulders and the fact that she’s being nagged by a sound tech. Carol doesn’t care about it. Whatever they’re discussing can wait. She walks closer and takes the plunge.

    “Zosia.”

    She doesn’t turn immediately, but she says something to the sound tech and he nods and swiftly leaves her alone. Then, after another beat of discouraging silence, she finally does turn and looks at Carol. It’s hard to read the frown on her face.

    “Carol,” she says, voice quieter, carrying none of the confidence she exuded on stage. “You’re… here.”

    Someone walks in the large, abyssal space between the two of them, oblivious to what is happening in Carol’s stomach, chest, mind. It’s a fucking mushroom cloud, in there.

    Before Carol can say anything, Zosia steps closer, takes her in, dragging her eyes over her with practiced ease. She bites her bottom lip, and Carol has a visceral reaction to that move, a primordial instinct to reach forward with her thumb and brush it against her. “Why?” She asks.

    “I fucked up,” Carol replies, eyes never leaving Zosia’s. She hopes she’ll see that she means it, but she only gets an eye roll in return. Definitely off to a shit start.

    “Clearly.”

    “I’m sorry.”

    “Are you?”

    “I should’ve asked you to stay,” Carol presses on, closing the distance between them – still not enough to touch her. Her gaze flicks down to Zosia’s mouth, just briefly, as she notices the smudged lipstick and longs to taste it like she’s fucking dying of thirst and Zosia’s spit might save her. “Actually, there are so many things I should’ve told you sooner.”

    Zosia exhales through her nose. She doesn’t say anything, but then another sound tech walks past them and she calls him over with a smile, all polite like she isn’t fuming because of Carol. She hands him her guitar and thanks him as he takes off with it. Once she’s free of that burden, she crosses her arms over her chest.

    “What makes you think I want to listen, now?”

    “I don’t know. Maybe I’m being stupid,” Carol mutters, then she takes a deep breath. “Why did you choose that song?”

    “What?”

    “This must be the place. Why did you sing it?”

    “It’s a good song.”

    “That’s not –” Carol’s hands instinctively flex at her sides. “Right.”

    “We played it every show,” Zosia says, sharper now, almost defensive. She stumbles over her next words. “It wasn’t for – I didn’t know you were coming. And how are you even here, exactly?”

    Carol hesitates. Does she have to be honest about everything?

    “I know people,” is all she says. A tiny white lie. She’ll come clean about Natalie if she doesn’t fuck the rest of it up. “Not the point. The thing is, I’m here for you. I’m here because I want – no, fuck that, I need you in my life, I need to be in your life and, if you’ll have me, I’ll make sure I prove to you that I can be more than the fucking idiot who couldn’t speak up. Hell, I’ll speak up every fucking day until you tell me to shut up.”

    Silence. Zosia just stares, and Carol knows she’s waiting for something more, something better. But the crease in her brow softens. Or maybe she’s just hallucinating.

    “I think about you all the time, Zosh. What you’re doing, who you’re doing it with. Fuck, sometimes I make lunch and think about whether you’ve eaten or not because you get so focused on other things that you forget about it. And you know what’s crazy? I did it long before we started this. I haven’t stopped thinking about you ever since you insulted me in the hallway. Which is pretty messed up, because you weren’t very nice.

    “And fuck, yes, I missed you. I never told you, but I did and I do and I will, if you decide that you don’t want anything to do with me anymore. I just didn’t know I could feel it this much, and it got me stuck in a bad place until I understood it.”

    “You’re an asshole,” Zosia mutters, lips tight and weirdly curling downwards. Her poker face completely slips away when Carol dares to brush her fingers against the palm of her hand. “You are.”

    “I know,” she replies, fingers bolder now that Zosia isn’t pulling back. Her voice breaks a bit, and she stops to catch her breath before speaking again. “I know I’m not the easiest person to be around. I can be a real pain in the ass, and maybe I don’t deserve another chance, because I can’t promise that I’ll change overnight, or that it’ll feel good all the time. But I’ll try. I want to.”

    “I can’t –” Zosia says, but a shaky laugh slips past her lips. “Do you really think this is enough to make us work? What’s the plan here, Carol?”

    “I don’t know. I’ll stop acting like an emotionally unavailable prick. I’ll talk about feelings, things, people. I won’t fight it anymore. And maybe you’ll do the same and we can meet in the middle. How is that not good enough?”

    “No, it’s – on paper, it’s good. It’s pretty fucking good,” Zosia closes her eyes, bracing herself, and opens them again when Carol asks her a question.

    “Do you remember when you told me you were leaving?”

    She nods.

    “I was about to say something right before that,” Carol says, blood rushing to her neck, crawling up over her cheeks. “And then I didn’t because I thought that you were proving my point, that you could leave me whenever, and I’d be back on my own, dealing with fucking feelings I never asked for. But I guess that was the thing. I dealt with them. I know what to call them now, and I’ll do it even if you don’t – even if this doesn’t mean anything to you anymore.”

    “It does. It means a lot.” Zosia’s lips parts, gaze dropping to Carol’s mouth.

    “Then can I say it now? What I wanted to say back then?”

    Zosia smiles. Carol can’t tell if it’s merely mirroring her own, but it’s there. It’s watery, wobbly, and it doesn’t feel like a door slammed to her face. It’s genuine, albeit guarded, like the eager steps a baby bird takes before plunging into the void and leaving the nest. “Yeah.”

    “I’m pretty fucking sure I’m in love with you too.”

    That’s it. She says those words and the world doesn’t fold on itself, it doesn’t explode. In fact, it keeps spinning with the added impulse of Zosia’s reaction.

    She lifts the hand Carol isn’t holding up to her tie, smirks when she feels the soft fabric between her fingers. It gets a choked sound out of her lips, the same old full-body shudder rushing through her the second she pulls and yanks her closer.

    “Well,” she whispers, breath hot on her face. “Took you long enough.”

    Then, Zosia presses her lips to Carol’s. Gentle, tentative, as if she’s giving her one last chance to pull away. Carol doesn’t. She knows now that she could never.

    More than a month of missing her, of replaying conversations and mistakes and possibilities condenses into one single point of contact that’s warm and familiar, but so different as well. Carol knows two things can be true at once, but right now it feels like she’s kissing Zosia for the first time, and she can’t imagine ever stopping.

    A quiet sound of relief slips out of Zosia’s throat as Carol’s arms circle her, holding her impossibly closer –
